No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.

Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.

I’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.

So yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.

Sometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.

It just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.

So… maybe guilt? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me most. Once I start, it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is standing off to the side going, Joan, stop, you have school tomorrow, this is enough. And then my hands just keep opening things anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because in my classroom I’m so organized—like, I can get twenty-eight kids lined up after recess, mostly, depending on the day—and at home I can’t even seem to stop myself from opening another box or jar. I’ll keep eating until I’m painfully full, like physically uncomfortable, and I still feel like I wasn’t really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.\n\nIt’s like this… the sensible teacher part of me is watching from somewhere outside my body, saying, Joan, stop, this is enough, you’re going to feel awful. And then my hands just keep opening something else, or putting more food in my mouth anyway. It’s almost embarrassing to say, because I can manage a classroom full of kids better than I can manage myself in my own kitchen.\n\nBy the time I kind of come back to myself, I’m usually painfully full. So… no, I don’t feel in control during it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the amount of food that you're eating during these episodes that makes you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.\n\nI’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.\n\nSo yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ve had a long day, but honestly most school days are long days, you know? Especially with the kids getting restless or someone crying because their glue stick “looked at them funny”—which sounds silly, but by 3 p.m. it really does feel like a lot.\n\nBut right before it happens… I don’t know if I’d say I always notice feeling stressed or anxious. Sometimes I’m just home and tired, doing normal evening things, and then it sort of… happens. I haven’t been great at paying attention to what I’m feeling right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or stressed, or is it more just because you're hungry or bored?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.\n\nIt just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.

Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.

My evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.

And at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.

So yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.

Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.

It’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. You don’t need more. Put it away.” But my hands just keep doing it. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.

And I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, and then I’m sitting there like, how did I let it get there again?

So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. I… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly. Maybe? I’m not sure.

I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sort of sit there and think, “What was that?” and then I get really quiet with myself, if that makes sense. Like I don’t want to look too closely at it.

But “ashamed” feels like a big word. I mean, I tell my students all the time that mistakes aren’t moral failures, and then of course I don’t apply that to myself, because apparently I’m only wise with seven-year-olds holding broken crayons. So… maybe there’s some of that. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.

Once I start, it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is standing off to the side going, Joan, stop, you have school tomorrow, this is enough. And then my hands just keep opening things anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because in my classroom I’m so organized—like, I can get twenty-eight kids lined up after recess, mostly, depending on the day—and at home I can’t even seem to stop myself from opening another box or jar. I’ll keep eating until I’m painfully full, like physically uncomfortable, and I still feel like I wasn’t really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.\n\nIt’s like this… the sensible teacher part of me is watching from somewhere outside my body, saying, Joan, stop, this is enough, you’re going to feel awful. And then my hands just keep opening something else, or putting more food in my mouth anyway. It’s almost embarrassing to say, because I can manage a classroom full of kids better than I can manage myself in my own kitchen.\n\nBy the time I kind of come back to myself, I’m usually painfully full. So… no, I don’t feel in control during it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the amount of food that you're eating during these episodes that makes you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.\n\nI’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.\n\nSo yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ve had a long day, but honestly most school days are long days, you know? Especially with the kids getting restless or someone crying because their glue stick “looked at them funny”—which sounds silly, but by 3 p.m. it really does feel like a lot.\n\nBut right before it happens… I don’t know if I’d say I always notice feeling stressed or anxious. Sometimes I’m just home and tired, doing normal evening things, and then it sort of… happens. I haven’t been great at paying attention to what I’m feeling right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or stressed, or is it more just because you're hungry or bored?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.\n\nIt just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.\n\nIt’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. You don’t need more. Put it away.” But my hands just keep doing it. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, and then I’m sitting there like, how did I let it get there again?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of yourself after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly. Maybe? I’m not sure.\n\nI definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sort of sit there and think, “What was that?” and then I get really quiet with myself, if that makes sense. Like I don’t want to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “ashamed” feels like a big word. I mean, I tell my students all the time that mistakes aren’t moral failures, and then of course I don’t apply that to myself, because apparently I’m only wise with seven-year-olds holding broken crayons. So… maybe there’s some of that. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8", "F50.4"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty after these episodes, looking back on what happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard for me to tell the difference between guilt and just this awful “ugh, why did I do that” feeling.

Sometimes I’ll look around afterward and it’s like when my classroom is a mess after an art project—glue sticks without caps, paper scraps everywhere, someone somehow got marker on the chair—and I know I have to clean it up, but it’s also this sinking feeling of, “How did it get this out of hand?” Except with this, it’s me, so it feels more personal.

But guilty… I don’t know. I don’t always have a clear thought like “I did something wrong.” It’s more like I want to move on really fast and not think about it too much.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten kind of out of control. Especially at night. I’ll be grading papers, and I start with just some leftovers, and then somehow I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… even snacks I packed for my classroom, which is embarrassing because then the next day I’m pretending I just “forgot” them. It’s like the amount of food scares me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I know I feel awful afterward, but “guilty” is kind of a loaded word, I guess. Like, sometimes I’m more just sitting there looking at the wrappers and bowls and thinking, how did that even happen?\n\nAnd then I start worrying about stupid practical things, like whether I’ll have enough snacks left for the kids’ reward bin, or if I need to stop at the store before school, and then I’m mad at myself for making the morning harder. So… maybe guilt? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me most. Once I start, it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is standing off to the side going, Joan, stop, you have school tomorrow, this is enough. And then my hands just keep opening things anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because in my classroom I’m so organized—like, I can get twenty-eight kids lined up after recess, mostly, depending on the day—and at home I can’t even seem to stop myself from opening another box or jar. I’ll keep eating until I’m painfully full, like physically uncomfortable, and I still feel like I wasn’t really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.\n\nIt’s like this… the sensible teacher part of me is watching from somewhere outside my body, saying, Joan, stop, this is enough, you’re going to feel awful. And then my hands just keep opening something else, or putting more food in my mouth anyway. It’s almost embarrassing to say, because I can manage a classroom full of kids better than I can manage myself in my own kitchen.\n\nBy the time I kind of come back to myself, I’m usually painfully full. So… no, I don’t feel in control during it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the amount of food that you're eating during these episodes that makes you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.\n\nI’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.\n\nSo yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ve had a long day, but honestly most school days are long days, you know? Especially with the kids getting restless or someone crying because their glue stick “looked at them funny”—which sounds silly, but by 3 p.m. it really does feel like a lot.\n\nBut right before it happens… I don’t know if I’d say I always notice feeling stressed or anxious. Sometimes I’m just home and tired, doing normal evening things, and then it sort of… happens. I haven’t been great at paying attention to what I’m feeling right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or stressed, or is it more just because you're hungry or bored?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.\n\nIt just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.\n\nIt’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. You don’t need more. Put it away.” But my hands just keep doing it. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, and then I’m sitting there like, how did I let it get there again?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of yourself after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly. Maybe? I’m not sure.\n\nI definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sort of sit there and think, “What was that?” and then I get really quiet with myself, if that makes sense. Like I don’t want to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “ashamed” feels like a big word. I mean, I tell my students all the time that mistakes aren’t moral failures, and then of course I don’t apply that to myself, because apparently I’m only wise with seven-year-olds holding broken crayons. So… maybe there’s some of that. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes, looking back on what happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard for me to tell the difference between guilt and just this awful “ugh, why did I do that” feeling.\n\nSometimes I’ll look around afterward and it’s like when my classroom is a mess after an art project—glue sticks without caps, paper scraps everywhere, someone somehow got marker on the chair—and I know I have to clean it up, but it’s also this sinking feeling of, “How did it get this out of hand?” Except with this, it’s me, so it feels more personal.\n\nBut guilty… I don’t know. I don’t always have a clear thought like “I did something wrong.” It’s more like I want to move on really fast and not think about it too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes, looking back on what happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard for me to tell the difference between guilt and just this awful “ugh, why did I do that” feeling.\n\nSometimes I’ll look around afterward and it’s like when my classroom is a mess after an art project—glue sticks without caps, paper scraps everywhere, someone somehow got marker on the chair—and I know I have to clean it up, but it’s also this sinking feeling of, “How did it get this out of hand?” Except with this, it’s me, so it feels more personal.\n\nBut guilty… I don’t know. I don’t always have a clear thought like “I did something wrong.” It’s more like I want to move on really fast and not think about it too much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Lately… pretty often, I guess. I’m trying to think of it like a calendar, because otherwise it all blurs together with school weeks and lesson planning and whether I remembered to buy more glue sticks—sorry.

For the past few months, I’d say there’s rarely been a week where it doesn’t happen at least one night. Sometimes it feels like I’ll have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of… try to balance it out. Like I won’t really eat at school, or I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, which is easy because lunch duty is chaos anyway. Or I’ll push myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga after work.

So… at least weekly, most weeks. Maybe that’s the simplest answer.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten kind of out of control. Especially at night. I’ll be grading papers, and I start with just some leftovers, and then somehow I’ve gone through cereal, crackers, peanut butter… even snacks I packed for my classroom, which is embarrassing because then the next day I’m pretending I just “forgot” them. It’s like the amount of food scares me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I know I feel awful afterward, but “guilty” is kind of a loaded word, I guess. Like, sometimes I’m more just sitting there looking at the wrappers and bowls and thinking, how did that even happen?\n\nAnd then I start worrying about stupid practical things, like whether I’ll have enough snacks left for the kids’ reward bin, or if I need to stop at the store before school, and then I’m mad at myself for making the morning harder. So… maybe guilt? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you start during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me most. Once I start, it’s like the reasonable teacher part of me is standing off to the side going, Joan, stop, you have school tomorrow, this is enough. And then my hands just keep opening things anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because in my classroom I’m so organized—like, I can get twenty-eight kids lined up after recess, mostly, depending on the day—and at home I can’t even seem to stop myself from opening another box or jar. I’ll keep eating until I’m painfully full, like physically uncomfortable, and I still feel like I wasn’t really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.\n\nIt’s like this… the sensible teacher part of me is watching from somewhere outside my body, saying, Joan, stop, this is enough, you’re going to feel awful. And then my hands just keep opening something else, or putting more food in my mouth anyway. It’s almost embarrassing to say, because I can manage a classroom full of kids better than I can manage myself in my own kitchen.\n\nBy the time I kind of come back to myself, I’m usually painfully full. So… no, I don’t feel in control during it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the amount of food that you're eating during these episodes that makes you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.\n\nI’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.\n\nSo yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ve had a long day, but honestly most school days are long days, you know? Especially with the kids getting restless or someone crying because their glue stick “looked at them funny”—which sounds silly, but by 3 p.m. it really does feel like a lot.\n\nBut right before it happens… I don’t know if I’d say I always notice feeling stressed or anxious. Sometimes I’m just home and tired, doing normal evening things, and then it sort of… happens. I haven’t been great at paying attention to what I’m feeling right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or stressed, or is it more just because you're hungry or bored?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.\n\nIt just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.\n\nIt’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I’m not sure. I don’t think I black out or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I was in the kitchen, I know I was eating, but afterward it can feel kind of blurry in the way a stressful school day blurs together.

Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? And then my hands just keep opening things anyway.\n\nIt’s weird because in my classroom I’m so organized—like, I can get twenty-eight kids lined up after recess, mostly, depending on the day—and at home I can’t even seem to stop myself from opening another box or jar. I’ll keep eating until I’m painfully full, like physically uncomfortable, and I still feel like I wasn’t really in charge of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know technically I’m the one doing it, but it doesn’t feel like I’m deciding each thing.\n\nIt’s like this… the sensible teacher part of me is watching from somewhere outside my body, saying, Joan, stop, this is enough, you’re going to feel awful. And then my hands just keep opening something else, or putting more food in my mouth anyway. It’s almost embarrassing to say, because I can manage a classroom full of kids better than I can manage myself in my own kitchen.\n\nBy the time I kind of come back to myself, I’m usually painfully full. So… no, I don’t feel in control during it.\n\nDoctor: Is it the amount of food that you're eating during these episodes that makes you feel like you've lost control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the amount is a big part of it. It’s not like I’m just having an extra bowl of cereal or something normal like that.\n\nI’ll start with, I don’t know, leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow it turns into cereal, crackers, peanut butter on a spoon, and then I’m digging into the snacks I packed for my classroom. Which is ridiculous, because those are supposed to be for the kids, like for little rewards or if someone forgot snack, and then I’m standing there thinking about whether I need to replace them before school.\n\nSo yes… the amount scares me. It feels like way more than I meant to eat, way more than makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ve had a long day, but honestly most school days are long days, you know? Especially with the kids getting restless or someone crying because their glue stick “looked at them funny”—which sounds silly, but by 3 p.m. it really does feel like a lot.\n\nBut right before it happens… I don’t know if I’d say I always notice feeling stressed or anxious. Sometimes I’m just home and tired, doing normal evening things, and then it sort of… happens. I haven’t been great at paying attention to what I’m feeling right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or stressed, or is it more just because you're hungry or bored?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I wish I had a neat answer, like “yes, it’s stress” or “no, I’m just hungry,” but I honestly haven’t figured that out.\n\nSometimes I think maybe I’m tired more than anything. After school my brain feels like it’s been making tiny decisions all day—who needs help, who’s about to throw a pencil, whose parent emailed—and then at night I’m just… sort of blank. But I don’t always feel upset in a clear way. And I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I’m bored, let me eat.\n\nIt just sort of starts, and then I’m already in it before I’ve labeled what I was feeling. So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.\n\nIt’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. You don’t need more. Put it away.” But my hands just keep doing it. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, and then I’m sitting there like, how did I let it get there again?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of yourself after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly. Maybe? I’m not sure.\n\nI definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sort of sit there and think, “What was that?” and then I get really quiet with myself, if that makes sense. Like I don’t want to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “ashamed” feels like a big word. I mean, I tell my students all the time that mistakes aren’t moral failures, and then of course I don’t apply that to myself, because apparently I’m only wise with seven-year-olds holding broken crayons. So… maybe there’s some of that. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes, looking back on what happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard for me to tell the difference between guilt and just this awful “ugh, why did I do that” feeling.\n\nSometimes I’ll look around afterward and it’s like when my classroom is a mess after an art project—glue sticks without caps, paper scraps everywhere, someone somehow got marker on the chair—and I know I have to clean it up, but it’s also this sinking feeling of, “How did it get this out of hand?” Except with this, it’s me, so it feels more personal.\n\nBut guilty… I don’t know. I don’t always have a clear thought like “I did something wrong.” It’s more like I want to move on really fast and not think about it too much.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… pretty often, I guess. I’m trying to think of it like a calendar, because otherwise it all blurs together with school weeks and lesson planning and whether I remembered to buy more glue sticks—sorry.\n\nFor the past few months, I’d say there’s rarely been a week where it doesn’t happen at least one night. Sometimes it feels like I’ll have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of… try to balance it out. Like I won’t really eat at school, or I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, which is easy because lunch duty is chaos anyway. Or I’ll push myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga after work.\n\nSo… at least weekly, most weeks. Maybe that’s the simplest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating some of the food during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I black out or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I was in the kitchen, I know I was eating, but afterward it can feel kind of blurry in the way a stressful school day blurs together.\n\nLike, I’ll remember pieces—opening something, standing at the counter, maybe putting a wrapper in the trash—but not in a neat order. It’s not like when a student asks me where their lunchbox is and I genuinely have no idea, because there are twenty-seven tiny emergencies happening at once. This is more… I was there, I just wasn’t paying attention to myself very well.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed “not remembering eating” exactly. More like not wanting to think about it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t think of it as “compensating” in the moment exactly, but that’s basically what it is.

After one of those nights, I’ll wake up and decide I’m not allowed to eat breakfast, and then at school I’ll skip lunch too. It’s easy to make excuses because there’s always something—copies jammed in the machine, someone crying because their pencil broke, a parent email I forgot to answer… so I can tell myself I’m just too busy.

And then later I’ll go out on the trail for way too long, or do extra yoga even if I’m already shaky and exhausted. Like I know my body is saying, “please stop,” but my head is saying I have to make up for it somehow.

So… maybe emotions are part of it, but I’m not sure I notice them at the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really clocked the speed of it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I know it feels kind of automatic, but whether I’m eating fast-fast or just not paying attention, I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll be standing in the kitchen, and then I’m thinking about how I need to laminate something for my class or whether I answered a parent email, and then suddenly more food is gone than I expected.\n\nSo… maybe? But I don’t think I’ve paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a very short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of why I finally came in, because it feels like an amount that scares me afterward.\n\nLike I’ll start with something normal, like leftovers while I’m grading papers, and then somehow I’ve also had cereal, crackers, peanut butter, and then I’m digging into the snacks I bought for my classroom. Which is embarrassing because those are supposed to be for the kids, or for emergencies, and then I’m mentally adding “buy more Goldfish” to my list like that’s the main problem.\n\nSo… yes, I think it is a lot in not a lot of time. It doesn’t feel normal when I look back at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are affecting your daily life or causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep trying to act like it’s just a night problem, like it happens and then the next day I’ll reset, but it kind of takes over everything around it.\n\nMy evenings end up being about… trying to fix it, I guess. Or planning around it. And then weekends too, when I should be catching up on grading or seeing people or doing laundry—actually my laundry has been ridiculous lately, there are like three baskets just sitting there—but instead I’m thinking about what happened with food, or what I’m going to do to make up for it.\n\nAnd at work I’m still functioning, like I show up and the kids get what they need, but I’m slower with grading and prep than I used to be. I feel like I’m spending so much energy trying to seem normal and put-together, because teachers are supposed to be, you know, calm and organized and have matching bins. And then with friends I’m kind of there but not really there. I’ll be listening, but part of me is still worrying about food or how I look or whether they can tell something’s off.\n\nSo yes. It’s distressing. More than I want to admit, probably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the scariest part.\n\nIt’s like once I start, there’s this reasonable teacher part of me—like the part that can manage twenty-eight kids arguing over scissors and still remember who needs their reading folder signed—that part is kind of standing off to the side going, “Joan, stop. You don’t need more. Put it away.” But my hands just keep doing it. Opening things, getting another spoon, going back to the cupboard, putting food in my mouth.\n\nAnd I’ll think, okay, after this bite I’m done, or after this one thing I’ll stop, and then I just… don’t. I keep going until I’m so full it hurts, and then I’m sitting there like, how did I let it get there again?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of yourself after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it shame exactly. Maybe? I’m not sure.\n\nI definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sort of sit there and think, “What was that?” and then I get really quiet with myself, if that makes sense. Like I don’t want to look too closely at it.\n\nBut “ashamed” feels like a big word. I mean, I tell my students all the time that mistakes aren’t moral failures, and then of course I don’t apply that to myself, because apparently I’m only wise with seven-year-olds holding broken crayons. So… maybe there’s some of that. I just don’t know how to separate shame from embarrassment or regret or just feeling really uncomfortable with myself afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes, looking back on what happened?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard for me to tell the difference between guilt and just this awful “ugh, why did I do that” feeling.\n\nSometimes I’ll look around afterward and it’s like when my classroom is a mess after an art project—glue sticks without caps, paper scraps everywhere, someone somehow got marker on the chair—and I know I have to clean it up, but it’s also this sinking feeling of, “How did it get this out of hand?” Except with this, it’s me, so it feels more personal.\n\nBut guilty… I don’t know. I don’t always have a clear thought like “I did something wrong.” It’s more like I want to move on really fast and not think about it too much.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Lately… pretty often, I guess. I’m trying to think of it like a calendar, because otherwise it all blurs together with school weeks and lesson planning and whether I remembered to buy more glue sticks—sorry.\n\nFor the past few months, I’d say there’s rarely been a week where it doesn’t happen at least one night. Sometimes it feels like I’ll have one of those nights where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I sort of… try to balance it out. Like I won’t really eat at school, or I’ll tell myself I’m too busy, which is easy because lunch duty is chaos anyway. Or I’ll push myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga after work.\n\nSo… at least weekly, most weeks. Maybe that’s the simplest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating some of the food during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I black out or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I was in the kitchen, I know I was eating, but afterward it can feel kind of blurry in the way a stressful school day blurs together.\n\nLike, I’ll remember pieces—opening something, standing at the counter, maybe putting a wrapper in the trash—but not in a neat order. It’s not like when a student asks me where their lunchbox is and I genuinely have no idea, because there are twenty-seven tiny emergencies happening at once. This is more… I was there, I just wasn’t paying attention to myself very well.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed “not remembering eating” exactly. More like not wanting to think about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for what you've eaten during these episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t think of it as “compensating” in the moment exactly, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up and decide I’m not allowed to eat breakfast, and then at school I’ll skip lunch too. It’s easy to make excuses because there’s always something—copies jammed in the machine, someone crying because their pencil broke, a parent email I forgot to answer… so I can tell myself I’m just too busy.\n\nAnd then later I’ll go out on the trail for way too long, or do extra yoga even if I’m already shaky and exhausted. Like I know my body is saying, “please stop,” but my head is saying I have to make up for it somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Maybe that’s the simplest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating some of the food during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I black out or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I was in the kitchen, I know I was eating, but afterward it can feel kind of blurry in the way a stressful school day blurs together.\n\nLike, I’ll remember pieces—opening something, standing at the counter, maybe putting a wrapper in the trash—but not in a neat order. It’s not like when a student asks me where their lunchbox is and I genuinely have no idea, because there are twenty-seven tiny emergencies happening at once. This is more… I was there, I just wasn’t paying attention to myself very well.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed “not remembering eating” exactly. More like not wanting to think about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for what you've eaten during these episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or restricting your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t think of it as “compensating” in the moment exactly, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll wake up and decide I’m not allowed to eat breakfast, and then at school I’ll skip lunch too. It’s easy to make excuses because there’s always something—copies jammed in the machine, someone crying because their pencil broke, a parent email I forgot to answer… so I can tell myself I’m just too busy.\n\nAnd then later I’ll go out on the trail for way too long, or do extra yoga even if I’m already shaky and exhausted. Like I know my body is saying, “please stop,” but my head is saying I have to make up for it somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever try to make up for what you've eaten during these episodes by purging or using laxatives?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Purging, like… making myself throw up? I’m not sure I want to get into the details wrong and then have it sound more definite than it is.

I’ve definitely done the “I’ll fix it tomorrow” thing, like we were saying, where I make rules for myself and then the whole next day at school becomes this weird math problem in my head. Which is ridiculous because I’m literally teaching kids fractions and then I’m doing my own secret, awful version of it in the background.

Laxatives… I don’t know. I mean, I know what you mean, but I haven’t really thought of it that way, if that makes sense. I guess I’m not sure.
